When he had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said, "Lord of Laṅkā, that question of yours about total purity is excellent! It is most excellent!"

Lord of Laṅkā, moreover it is like this. When the Blessed One, the purity of essential nature, was naturally dwelling in the buddhafield called Totally Pure, then, at that time, he regarded whatever appeared to the immeasurable victors, whose intentions were completely gathered together, as the sphere of total purity. That appearance of suchness I shall discuss thoroughly and without error.
Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:
As gold lost in a dark pit appears of itself by the light of a lamp,
So mind, lost in the darkness of ignorance, appears of itself.
This root of the complete gathering of intentions
Was taught by gathering the intentions of those who passed in former ages.
The root of all phenomena is gathered in this very mind.
Mind itself, transformed, appears as the five wisdoms.
Since it is definitively placed in that very meaning,
The transformation of mind itself is also just that.
Since the five wisdoms are complete in it,
The five wisdoms too are gathered into one.
When the nature of mind is realized just as it is,
Primordially buddha, unmistaken and uncontrived,
It is called the gathering, since one has attained the ground that evenly pervades.
At that stage the views of existence and nonexistence come to an end.
How the suchness of mind is,
And whether its present continuity exists or not, should be known.
How, here, should one look at suchness?
Is it appearing or not appearing?
Then is it buddha or not buddha?
Apart from it, is there a buddha or not?
Decide through deliberation how it is to be posited.
Even when, having ascertained it thus, it has become so,
Do not suppress the adventitious path of action, but practice it likewise.
Therefore the nature of all is gathered in that very thing.
If one rests there without thought and without reference point,
Whatever appears and whatever exists is signless and one;
That very thing, without arising and ceasing, is primordially buddha.
Since that very thing is undistracted, even without clearing anything away,
One rides upon completion as a whole and has attained the very ground from the beginning.
Even when that very thing becomes clear in experience, as experience and warmth,
In order to show that it does not conflict with the doors of all phenomena,
One blocks the doors to deviation through possessing the three samādhis,
And through the three great warmths one experiences the sacred intention.
Therefore, by finding the experience of the three samādhis,
The fruit, the nature of the six perfections, is complete.
Rest mind itself, uncontrived, in the state of space.
Then, whatever memories and thoughts arise,
Since they are resolved by uncontrived suchness itself,
It is naturally uncontrived and lightly luminous:
So it is praised in the Guhyasamāja.
Undistracted, without arising and ceasing, that itself is samādhi;
The meaning of the three yogas is complete at that point.
Therefore, through the lineage of intention of the spiritual friend of all,
One should know mind itself as the ultimate meaning.
Since that itself is Vajrasattva,
Whoever sees this sees his face.
Since from seeing that onward one is sacred,
One is therefore renowned as the spiritual friend of all.
Whoever has seen such a meaning
Should look at nothing else, and be devoted to that.
Then, ascertaining that what is to be done is done and there is nothing to seek,
That is called totally pure self-arisen wisdom.
Thereby, whatever pleasure and pain of grasping and attachment arise,
One affirms that the two accumulations are complete without being accumulated.
Nonconceptual samādhi, with signs completely purified,
Dharmatā made manifest, great qualities unceasing:
Since the two obscurations are purified and the great accumulations complete,
One attains the grounds with all their factors in accord.
This is the profound intention and conduct of the all-gathering;
It is not the sphere of everyone.
Just as one who sees sees its meaning,
So, because the deluded do not see it,
The darkness of clinging from the beginning is hard to clear away.
Though it is said that there are distinctions between ground and ground,
When the nonduality of seeing and not seeing is clear,
The sacred intention is certainly spontaneously present.
What difference is there in nature between empty and unborn?
Existence and nonexistence, permanence and annihilation, action and seeking, and the rest,
All primordially abide as spontaneously present.
Whatever cause and fruit appear, whatever exists,
They appear as the ornament of dharmatā, of suchness.
That itself, unobstructed, is the mode of total completion.
Even those who say that higher and lower and the rest, the two, faults and qualities,
And cause and effect are clear as separate,
Through mere conditions of seeing and not seeing, of hope and effort,
All come down to one in suchness.
Just as in it there is primordially no meeting and parting,
Whatever unequal and equal spheres appear,
There is nothing at all that is not this very suchness.
That itself, in that, unites the completion of the accumulations, means, and wisdom.
When this is ascertained, the gathered intentions of the sacred ones
Are gathered in that ultimate, uncontrived mind itself.
Since the nature of mind is unmade and self-arisen,
It is called samādhi, free of all reference points.
For that mind of awakening ascertained in this way,
Since there is no place to travel to and no form of view,
Signs, omniscience, faults and qualities are primordially absent too.
Both Great Vehicles are complete, all doubts are cut,
And it is resolved in the unexcelled Great Perfection.
Therefore the subtle aspect that distinguishes extremes and middle,
In that very distinguishing of extremes and middle, all is one;
For the sake of a distinctly exalted meaning it is called the all-gathering.
As for objects outer and inner, such as desire and action, held as causes,
Abiding as mere signs of joy,
And those through whose conduct phenomena are gathered into mind,
Even those who say that the two extremes are naturally clear,
Because they are seen as resolved, do not exist or not exist apart.
If one holds that they exist, that is a great fault.
Therefore, whatever separate meditative concentrations appear,
They are mere delusive appearances, the sphere of childish beings.
The sacred and the inferior are like an illusion and a mirage;
Since by nature they are without arising and ceasing, they are like space.
There, in the all-gathering, there is no seeing for anyone.
Some hold that not appearing at all is faultless;
That is a delusion, and so it is not acceptable.
Seen and unseen appearances have no intrinsic nature.
The profound intention and conduct of the secret all-gathering,
When self-arisen self-awareness appears directly,
Is called the seeing of self-arisen wisdom itself.
When the sacred intention is seen in this way,
All thought and attachment are cleared, beyond the objects of seeing:
It is resting without action, without appearance, without seeking.

With no object of reference, unattached to phenomena, it is abandoned.
The expanse where what is seen and felt, the path of bliss, lies open without limit:
Whatever changes and whatever appears has never moved from that anywhere.
Its reasoning is made clear by suchness itself.
The all-gathering does not examine its meaning.
All phenomena appear as self-arisen, self-aware wisdom.
Their meaning is unborn, unarisen, unborn,
And even what is designated as cause and fruit is primordially absent.
Whoever knows it thus has completed the two accumulations in that very knowing.
To rest evenly in the meaning with a contrived mind is itself a fault:
It conflicts with that, and does not meet with it.
But letting the certainty that comes from that pervade
Does not conflict with it; that itself is certainty.
At the time of self-awareness, there is no duality in it.
That very essence is spontaneously present as cause and fruit.
At that time ultimate experience and warmth are complete.
Appearing and not appearing, permanence and annihilation, and the rest:
Through certainty in experience and warmth one reaches the depth of the meaning,
And this doubt about separate positions is cut.
If, firmly devoted to that very thing, with certainty and a stable mind,
One settles the view through connection with it,
One who has a sacred rank in an inferior body
Is a mighty lord, so bow to him with respect.
With the five great provisions free of deceit,
Regard him like the jewel on the crown of Indra.
The three words of the complete gathering of intentions
Should be made the confidence of the mind through faith and certainty.
Certainty in the meaning of the three words is found and becomes manifest.
The definitive meaning comes down to self-awareness alone.
When the Lord of Secrets had spoken thus, the Lord of Laṅkā and the other beings with inferior bodies came to a definitive settlement on the root of all phenomena.
From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, this is the fifty-sixth chapter, teaching the sphere of pure appearance of the root of all phenomena.
Beginning the ninth section.
Then again the Lord of Laṅkā and the other bodhisattvas, great beings with inferior bodies, put a thorough question to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, when we hear that you here, in what was set out as the ten categories of Dharma, said that one should leave the nature uncontrived, how should that be fully understood? What is the nature? In what way is it uncontrived? Of what is that the nature?" When they had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said:
"O friends with inferior bodies, the nature is that which does not change. Uncontrived means genuine. These are the nature of secret mantra, the nature of the view of it, the nature of not transgressing it, and the nature of conducting oneself accordingly.
"Friends, here the nature of secret mantra is as follows:
Because its nature is like space,
Free of conceptual thought, it does not conceive anything.
Likewise, because its nature is that of earth,
Its nature is that from which all arises.
Because it is like the nature of fire,
It makes all entities nonexistent.
Because it is like the nature of wind,
It clears away all moving dust and unmoving dust.
Because it is just the nature of water,
With nothing else, it is gathered into suchness.
Because it is the nature of medicine,
It draws out the chronic disease of saṃsāra.
Because it is thus the nature of nourishment,
It is the sustenance of all pure livelihood.
Because it is like the nature of cutting,
It cuts through the ocean of suffering.
Because its nature is like the sun,
It illuminates the dark house, showing what is there.
Because its nature is like Mount Meru,
It cannot be shaken or moved by anything.
Because its nature is like the ocean,
Blessing arises from the unmoving.
Likewise, because its nature is that of elixir,
It is just as when poison turns into medicine.
These and other infinite supreme qualities,
Free of conceptual thought, all arise from it;
Uncontrived and uncorrupted, it is just so.
Friends, these should be known as the nature of secret mantra." This is how what they had heard was discussed.
"Friends, what then is the nature of the view of it? It is as follows:
Unmistaken, unchanging, decisively determined,
Unattached, nonconceptual, unshaken.
For example, from the nature of space,
Clouds, mist, haze, and vapor,
The moon, the sun, and the hosts of stars
Appear, yet it remains just like space.
Likewise, from the space of suchness,
Whatever conceptual thoughts of signs arise,
The yogin should see
That dharmatā remains just like space.
In that suchness, uncontrived just as it is,
Through the difference between seeing and not seeing,
Look at the differences of view one by one.
For the conceptual it is conceptual thought itself;
For those with wrong craving it is wrong thought itself;
For those who merely hold to a position it is a substance of convention;
For those who know positions it appears as an illusion.
Certain of that itself there, one sees space;
Abiding like space, in space,
It merely appears as space itself.
Friends, these should be known as the nature of the view." This is how what they had heard was definitively discussed.
"Friends, what is the nature of not transgressing it? It is as follows:
Here, in dharmatā, which is like space,
Where all is entirely one,
Free of the alternatives of guarding and not guarding,
How could there be a measure to hold for guarding?
The yogin who is like space
Guards the samaya that is like space.
If here there is no boundary to guard, there is ease;
By that, everything is guarded.
Why? Because each and every thing
Is ascertained as sacred,
Holding to measures and having limits is itself the breach.
Better that those conceptions
Of guarding and not guarding be absent;
To hold to such boundaries of guarding
Is an illusion, and therefore sacred.
Who would call that guarding?
There are no words for guarding and not guarding.
To accept and reject within dharmatā,
How could that be sacred?
Phenomena of guarding and not guarding,
Since there is nothing to guard, are left just as they are.
In the ultimate of suchness,
One places the mind just as it is,
And by the samaya that is beyond expression,
Binds oneself willingly where there is no boundary to guard.
This samaya of space, with nothing to guard,
In a nature according to how it is known,
Appears separately as vow and as moral discipline.
For worldly people it is binding law;
For children it is knowing proper manners;
For those come of age it is known as a vow;
For the unexcelled supreme it appears as samaya.
Though it has not moved from a single meaning,
It is known separately according to those to be trained.
Being endowed with suchness, it is sacred;
If one transgresses it, it becomes a fault.
This great samaya of the sacred,
In which guarding one is guarding all,
Should not be made to conflict with anything,
And should be made to serve the purpose of whatever it is for.
Friends, these should be known as the nature of not transgressing." This is how what they had heard was thoroughly discussed.

"O friends, what then is the nature of conducting oneself accordingly? It is as follows:
When the three extremes of body, speech, and mind
Are completely purified and perfected,
Since that is sacred conduct,
It is explained as knowing the sacred family, not the inferior.
As for the conduct of vajra body,
Since there is nowhere to go, one does not contrive the body.
Since one is alone, one abides as one.
Like the gaze of an elephant,
One should likewise abide in a natural way.
Wherever one abides, one abides always,
Yet one should not be attached to that abiding.
In whichever of the four modes of conduct one is engaged,
One should abide uncontrived and natural.
Apart from merely protecting against harm,
So that one does not fall into distress,
One should not contrive for beauty or adornment;
Wherever one is, apply oneself to whatever brings ease.
As for the sphere of vajra mantra,
The yogin who is without activity
Does not perform the work of stūpas,
Does not read volumes of scripture,
Does not engage in the work of making images,
Does not bind mudrās with the hands;
Verbal recitation obscures that.
The yogin does not prostrate with the body,
Does not look to gain and honor,
Not for profit, not for fame.
Why? Because these are outer distraction,
Because they are extreme distraction,
And are not close to that.
So one should keep this body in its natural state.
For the yogin of definitive meditative concentration,
The food of vajra mantra
Is merely a support that sustains the body's vitality.
Apart from what comes along and what is available,
One should not strive after food for its own sake.
How could it be for the sake of fine taste and flavor?
Eat just as it is, uncontrived and natural;
Do not contrive for the sake of livelihood.
There is no rejecting or adopting of attachment or nonattachment.
One should not take what is given,
And should not resort to living by begging.
Why? Because that does not transform it;
It is a livelihood not completely pure.
To draw a boundary out of the boundless:
This is the work of the yogin.
When, for the sake of yoga, one enters
The inner dharmatā, what is to be known,
In order to remove the interruptions
Caused by the distractions of outer busyness,
At the boundary between outer and inner
One should cut off all coming and going and moving about.
Since for this there is no ground of abiding and no support,
To contrive the body for comfortable abiding is beyond compare in fault.
To contrive one's mode of conduct out of clinging:
That is to corrupt the body by contrivance.
Do not contrive bodily solitude for meditative concentration.
That which is solitary there, uncontrived,
Is itself the place of primordial solitude.
This is the domain of the supreme yogin.
Do not contrive mountain caves and celestial palaces.
That itself, immeasurable and uncontrived,
Is the primordially wondrous palace,
Self-arisen without being made.
As for the sphere of vajra speech,
Apart from speaking pleasantly for the sake of the sacred,
There is no chatter or joking.
Everything said is definitive;
One speaks straightforwardly and does not alter one's words.
As one has spoken, so one should act.
One does not talk about everything at all;
Whatever one says should be connected with the meaning.
Except for the occasion and the purpose,
One should not ramble.
Illustrate everything with examples and bring it home;
Make everything measured, a valid cognition.
The yogin in the uncontrived state:
This is explained as the conduct of vajra speech.
As for the sphere of vajra mind:
Uncontrived, unmoving, free of thought,
Undistracted, not dull, it remains clear;
Unattached to desire, free of superimposition and denial,
With nothing to adopt or reject, without attachment or aversion,
Without concealment or destruction, it is not gain or wealth;
Free of sides, limitless, completely pure,
Having donned the armor, it goes straight upon the path itself.
Poison is transformed and kept as medicine; it is equipped with weapons;
It is devoted to the vajra fortress;
It has the intelligence of a wish-fulfilling jewel.
Where it does not change, what does not change,
Being one with all in this expanse,
Because it is manifest to the mind,
It is completely beautiful and conscientious,
And through the power of that itself it has a sense of shame.
Since in it there is no I or no I,
It is completely endowed with everything,
And certain through the bliss of everything.
Because this is empty, it conceives nothing.
Because that itself is peace, it is without turmoil,
And because nothing at all arises from anywhere,
It does not search elsewhere,
And without material concerns, it does not contrive its manner.
Because there is no torment, there is no desire;
Because it arises unattached, it holds to contentment.
Because it does not grasp anything as a sign,
There is no clean or unclean, and there are no three times.
Likewise there is no sacred one either.
Mind itself, that essence of space,
Is massed over and covered by adventitious clouds.
Without scattering them with the wind of wisdom,
To wish to clear the obscurations and make dharmatā clear
Through conduct like that of a clown
Is like this: when the sun is completely obscured
By a shadow like clouds made of moisture,
Childish ones, who do not know its nature,
Blow with the breath of their mouths
At the heap of the great gathered clouds,
Wishing to clear the clouds and make the sunlight clear.
Friends, these should be known as the nature of conducting oneself accordingly." This is what was heard without error.
From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, this is the fifty-seventh chapter, called the chapter on the nature of yoga.
Then again the Lord of Laṅkā and the other bodhisattvas, great beings, put a thorough question to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, how should we fully understand what you taught as a pledge, that one should grow a tree? Of what is that the tree?" When they had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said:
"O friends, the tree is the tree of awakening. Because it ripens into the unexcelled fruit and becomes the nourishment of all without exception, one should grow that tree.
"Friends, it is as follows:
From that very vajra essence,
In that where all thorns are cleansed,
In what is entirely and completely perfected,
What was explained on the occasion of application,
Which definitively comments on its intention,
That itself should be set forth.
There, in commenting on the intention, there are ten occasions of application:
Generating aspiration and applying effort,
Entering the training and the transformation of perception,
After applying, entering the object of experienced taste,
The great realization, gaining confidence, and becoming workable:
These are the ten occasions of mind together with continuity.
Because those essences possess continuity,
Through the ten occasions of the great yoga,
In the distinctions of the five kinds of yoga,
Sixteen are definitely completed through continuity.
Likewise, in all wisdoms,
Through the distinctions of ten kinds,
From the very first until completion,
Whatever is applied causes the obscurations to set.
Wherever a person sees,
There a single awareness, the mind of awakening, arises.
If one asks what it is that sets,
It is moving, aspiring, arising,
Applying, entering, and not changing;
Awareness, abiding, and completion.

Whatever image of whatever thing appears,
These are examples that accord with insubstantial self-appearance:
It appears like subtle, ordinary, and great stars,
And like the moon of the second day,
Of the third, the fourth, and the fifth,
Of the eighth, and of the sixteenth.
On the occasions when intention arises,
The mind in which moving predominates comes forth;
Like a gloomy mass of dark clouds,
Darting flickers and stars appear dimly.
On the occasions when effort is applied,
The mind in which aspiring predominates comes forth;
It is like very ordinary stars,
Dim wherever they are.
On the occasions when one enters the training,
The arising mind of awakening comes forth;
Like great flares of fire
And great stars, it appears dimly.
On the occasions when perception is transformed,
The mind in which applying predominates is born;
It appears like a whirling firebrand's wheel
And like the moon of the first day.
On the occasions when one enters the great yoga,
The mind in which entering predominates is born;
It appears like a lamp
And like the moon of the second day.
On the occasions when the taste is experienced,
The mind in which joy predominates comes forth;
It appears like a lamp within a vessel,
Or like the third day's moon.
On the occasions when one engages with objects,
The mind in which not changing predominates comes forth;
It is close to attainment and to being shown,
And its nature appears like the fourth day's moon.
On the occasions of great examination,
The mind in which awareness predominates comes forth;
The first great sign is closely approached,
And its nature appears like the fifth day's moon.
On the occasions when confidence is gained,
The mind in which abiding predominates comes forth;
It appears like a perfectly steady lamp,
Its nature like the eighth day's moon.
When the signs are obtained, it is called accomplished.
On the occasions when it becomes workable,
The mind in which completion predominates comes forth;
It appears like a lamp that needs no remedy,
And like the moon complete in all its digits.
Through certain stability it is accomplishment.
Mind itself is buddha from the very beginning,
But, obscured by conceptuality, one does not know it.
From the tree of the mind of awakening
The unsurpassed fruit ripens.
Therefore, excellent friends,
The mind of awakening of the excellent ones
Is at all times possessed of continuity;
To be without continuity, cut off, is not excellent.
Through continuity itself it surely ripens:
The growing tree where it ripens
Is the excellent tree of awakening,
And the excellent fruit will surely ripen.

Friends, this has been the definitive discussion of the intention of growing the excellent tree.

This concludes the fifty-eighth chapter, on growing the excellent tree, the secret of reality, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the bodhisattva great beings, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, asked a question: "O Lord of Secrets, concerning the intention behind the vow, 'One should follow the excellent ones,' how is one to follow the excellent ones? How is that to be fully understood?"

When they had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "O friends, it is like this: following the excellent ones means the excellent path that is known with certainty. Friends, what is the excellent path? It is this. On one occasion that I saw and heard, the excellent spiritual friend himself declared definitively: 'Because the body of essential nature has no remedy, it is endowed with all conceptualizations: permanence, annihilation, arising, cessation, objectification, attachment, aversion, delusion, pride, and jealousy; the five objects of desire; killing, taking what is not given, sexual misconduct, lying, slander, harsh speech, idle talk, covetousness, malice, and wrong view. To engage with certainty in the five deeds of immediate retribution, the five that are close to them, the four grave ones, the eight perversions, defiled meat, excrement, urine, blood, semen, and so forth, is to be known as the excellent path of the excellent ones.'

"When he had declared this with excellent certainty, I said to him:
'Emaho! A wondrous, marvelous Dharma!
From the true and authoritative speech
Of the perfect buddhas who proclaim it,
How does this origin of perversity come about?'
When I had made this request with earnest longing,
The excellent spiritual friend declared:
'Because the supreme means are beyond measure,
For those attached to what is perverse,
In order to bring them to the definitive meaning,
This is taught as a method of guiding them.
However firmly they hold to perverse attachments,
Those who would not enter but would turn away,
If they are drawn in by the method of perverse conduct,
Will without doubt come forth.
In order to catch them by the method of enticement,
According to their various individual inclinations,
It is taught as a method of drawing them in to rely on it,
Matched so as to accord with their views and conduct.
For example, just as parents, by the means
Of threats and frightening, urge
Naughty children to the work they ought to do,
So out of love one leads them to the aim.
Moreover, just as someone
Who wishes to go to the far shore of a river
Travels relying on a boat,
And, having reached the far side, abandons it,
So the different methods or vehicles,
Though they are means of guidance to be relied on,
The moment the certainty of the meaning is found,
Are discarded like the boat once the river is crossed.
Therefore, if suchness is known,
Everything whatsoever, without exception,
Being certain as the excellent path,
Is the shortcut among paths.'
Having said this, he remained in silence.

"O friends, therefore, if you enter this excellent path that follows the excellent ones, then everything, in every respect, in all its aspects, will become known, clear, unconfused, and fully complete. Those who wish to fully understand saṃsāra should also know this path. Those who wish to fully understand nirvāṇa should also know this path. Furthermore, those who wish to attain the twenty great miracles should also fully know this excellent path. Those who wish to level the eighty mountains of conceptuality should also fully know this excellent path. Those who wish to cut off the nine doors of downfall should also fully know this excellent path.

Whoever wish to know all these and the rest,
Limitless, each and every one, complete,
Should come to know this excellent path.
Mind itself is the excellent path:
Like the path of a bird in the sky, it leaves no trace,
The excellent path without fear or deviation.
This definitive path, too,
So that all may come to know it,
Is indicated by the path of words;
Yet it is inexpressible, beyond the reach of words.
In its own nature, that itself,
The uncontrived nature, is suchness.
It has no name, but because names are imputed,
Seeing and not seeing become distinct.
From that it becomes the basis for conduct:
So that through expression that very thing may be seen,
From the enumerations of the path of words
The doors of the path of words become measureless.
From that comes the trail cut by the excellent ones,
The scripture that arose from their minds,
The search for the treasure close at hand by lamplight,
The shortcut, excellent awakening, which is this:
If one follows after whatever arises,
One will circle on the wheel of saṃsāra.
If one blocks whatever movement arises,
It becomes a mere cessation of the continuum.
The fool for whom nothing arises
Enters the dark path of the extremists.
Whatever one grasps singly as "this"
Is a mountain of conceptualizing signs.
If one sets a limit, saying, "There is no before and after,"
That is the extremist of nonexistence and annihilation.
Whatever stirs for a single moment within the uncontrived,
Where arising and ceasing take place,
Whatever arises and grows from that
Makes the path of enumerations grow,
But the excellent path itself is not found.
For example, childish beings
Abandon their shadow and seek something pure;
By the very thing from which it came,
Though they search, they find no waning or waxing.
That itself is thus from the very beginning.
Whoever here seeks the path
Through various sufferings, to the point of exhaustion,
Does pointless work, the work of māras.
Whatever is said of it in words
Is like arguing with the sky.
Though medicine and treatment are explained in words,
They do not heal the crowd of the sick.
If one has not become that very suchness,
The path of words that one speaks is not it.
Here, to abandon one thing and seek another
Is like abandoning a form to seek its shadow.
Since mind itself is the excellent path,
Ignorance and wisdom are not distinct.
Nonconceptuality is one in this path.
Since mind itself appears as variety,
Variety is one in this path.
For example, from a single material, gold,
Shapes and forms are made distinct,
And so each of them bears its own name;
Whatever they are called, they are one in the essence of gold.
Likewise, in the nature of mind,
Through the difference of seeing and not seeing,
However one looks, so it appears;
And though it appears, nothing other than it is observed.
Just as a jewel that blazes like a lamp,
Though it lies hidden in a foul pit,
Because its nature is self-appearing,
Shines its light clearly into the sky,
So the jewel lamp of mind itself,
Though sunk in the mire of a wretched body,
Being self-arisen, self-pacified, self-appearing,
Is fully luminous in its uncontrived nature.
This mind is just like space:
All-pervading, empty and clear, it gives rise to variety.
Arising, being felt, moving, and ceasing, all of these
Arise by nature and subside by themselves.
In short, just as one examines and thinks,
So it changes, and thus all appears as its essence.
From the uncontrived, motionless itself
The causes of variety arise without fault,
And the miraculous display is unceasing and without fear.
It comes down simply to resting the mind, undistracted,
In a nature like that of space.
That is the great purpose, accomplished without toil.
That itself is the essence
From which the buddhas of all three times arise.
However many abide in its meaning,
If, through seeing, they grow accustomed to that very thing there,
Finding the path, seeing the path, experience and warmth
Will certainly arise through the meaning itself;
And even for one who is not such, once it is grasped,
Excellent awakening will arise.
Toward phenomena that are conceptualized signs,
Certain understanding arises.
That all phenomena are like this,
And yet not to see it so, is a great wonder;
To see it so is also a great wonder.
Emaho! One is moved to wonder, "What is this?"
Those who do not see that itself,
Through the great toil of difficult, effortful achievement,
Seek the excellent path, each path a long one;
Though its meaning arises and is present,
They do not find that path.
To find the path of the excellent ones
Is more excellent than excellence itself.
It is not the equal of supports and images;
Peace with a body remaining and final cessation, these two are not its equal;
The grounds of training are not its equal;
Yogic mentation, the extremes of conceptuality,
And whatever is equal to such things are not its equal.
Therefore, since the great pure path
Is this secret of mind,
By knowing this secret path
Gathered from all intentions,
There is no reason for the work of searching:
By seeing this very path,
Without any hardship or effort,
Each and every thing is accomplished.
Wherever the secret of mind abides,
That is the excellent solitude;
As the supreme great hermitage itself
The Gathering of the Intentions of All has declared it.
That itself is the immeasurable supreme celestial palace,
The marvelous mansion of the wish-fulfilling jewel,
Unmade, self-arisen, without outside or inside,
Become inward through and through.
Whatever gathers here is no assembly;
In it, that itself is the supreme gathering of the assembly.
Gathering all phenomena, it has no arising,
The supreme delight of immeasurable Victors.
Whatever here is enjoyment of the senses is a requisite;
That itself is the great requisite.
If this superior requisite is known,
It is the root of all requisites.
To make meditation is not meditation;
What is to be contemplated has no reference point.
If this excellent secret path is known,
This itself is the excellent path of meditation.
Though one says "guard," nothing to be guarded is observed;
This secret path is emptiness, arising without existence.
If one knows the words through the greatness of center and extremes,
This is the excellent secret of guarding.
The path of words speaks of equality, yet equality is not observed;
In this secret where all is one,
How could there be anything more to call "equal"?
It is equal to the Gathering of the Intentions of All.
Whatever else is the conduct of discipline,
Bending toward that and engaging in its play,
Since here all is tamed and here all has entered,
The Gathering of All is declared the supreme discipline.
This path, the very reality, the secret of mind,
Being accomplished from the beginning, has no supplication or obtaining;
"Accomplishment that is never interrupted,"
So the excellent Gathering of the Intentions of All declares.
Thus, if one sees the secret of mind,
By seeing the path not seen before,
Seeing is nothing whatsoever;
That to which it appears is the path of seeing.

Friends, this has been the full discussion of what was heard in this way, called "the excellent path that follows the excellent ones."

This concludes the fifty-ninth chapter, on following yoga, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the heroic friends, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, put a question to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, how are we to fully understand the intention behind the vow, 'Here one should keep the blazing jewel lamp'?"

When they had put this question, the Lord of Secrets said: "O friends, the blazing jewel is a source of light. Since it is not extinguished even by gusts of scattering wind, and does nothing but dispel darkness, it is called a lamp. It is this: the knowing that dispels unknowing. Just as in the heart of the sun or in a jewel lamp there is no ground for darkness, so in the nature of knowing there is no ground for unknowing.

"Moreover, when the excellent lamp has set, distinct forms appear within the single reality of all, namely: the knowing of darkness, the knowing of the astonished, the knowing of the childish, the knowing of partial understanding, the knowing that distinguishes extremes and center, and the knowing of excellence itself.

"O friends, the knowing of darkness is knowing this wrongly. The knowing of those with perverse craving speaks of difference and holds to duality. The childish are those who speak of nonexistence yet hold that everything exists. It is like this:
From particles to leagues, from instants
To eons, stars, sun and moon,
Mountains, the encircling ring, and the bodies of the embodied,
Mind and life span, the measures of enjoyments,
And the characteristics of time, abiding, destruction and setting,
They break all of these down
Into separate particulars,
And speak of nonexistence while holding that things exist.
Because their inclination is small, they go no further.
The yogin here who understands in part
Holds that, since all this is only here,
It appears as insubstantial consciousness itself;
That itself, uncontrived, is suchness,
And because it exists, he holds the dharmakāya to be permanent.
The yogins who distinguish definitively
Act on the conventional level,
Since things appear with the character of illusion,
And make no definitive division.
Some are certain of it in this way:
Apart from that, it does not come about;
Since that itself, by that, is insubstantial,
It does not exist as the ultimate.
Some, since nothing is grasped,
Allow that its appearing may be anywhere at all:
The subtlest ground, knowing all aspects,
Does not exist in the way of a deceiver.
What is the excellence of excellence itself?
In the nature of reality, buddha from the beginning,
In order to reverse views other than this,
One says, "buddha from the beginning";
But this is said merely as a convention.
"From the beginning" is a path of words, spoken;
For what is free of the two conventions
One does not apply the terms of words that superimpose or deny.
The conventions of speech pass beyond the path of words.
"Does this space exist or not?"
Fools will argue so;
The wise are not like that;
To argue over it is a great wonder.
"Does this suchness exist or not?"
The childish will argue so;
Those persons who are not like that know
That argument is nothing but corrupted wisdom.
All phenomena that appear in this way
Are buddha from the beginning, by their very nature;
They do not turn from what they are not into what they are.
In the reality of essential nature,
What is there to make of "it is" and "it is not"?
"This space does not exist":
Whoever does not know conceives it so;
Whoever knows does not conceive it from the beginning;
He does not conceive it as not being, and speaks no words.
In cause, action, and enumeration,
In characteristic, certainty, and essence,
In result and purpose, each one arises.
Thus, here, whatever is the cause
Is self-arising itself, with examination and analysis;
Whatever spreads forth as sense sources in whatever,
These two themselves are the cause.
Whatever is and is not from whatever, however many,
The distinctions, particulars and differences,
With definitive distinguishing, engagement, relation and application,
Holding to them without error,
Manifesting, intended meaning, and connection.
The enumerations of two kinds are according to the meaning.
It makes certain of all.
That itself is unmixed and fully complete:
Insubstantial, nonconceptual, fully complete,
Each one distinct, inconceivable,
Unmixed, it acts in accord with how things are.
Whatever here is without error,
Fully perfected, is the supreme itself.
Wholly unconfused, it illuminates everything;
Wherever it is, it is unobstructed and unimpeded;
Entering all, it cuts through ignorance;
It is what has become all the sense sources.
Whatever spreads forth, into which the wind of whatever enters,
Is the immeasurable wisdom of the wise.
Wherever there is reversal, that itself is pure.
Because that itself has reached perfection,
With the lamp that illuminates the target
One should look upon that itself in it.
One who does not know the light of the lamp
Is veiled by darkness from everything to be seen;
Nothing will ever appear,
And he goes to the great city of the blind.
Whoever keeps the blazing lamp,
Through which darkness is cleared from all and all is illuminated,
Will see the unmistaken, uncontrived suchness
Through the lamp's aim upon the target.
Thus, excellent friends,
Without keeping the fire of the blazing jewel lamp
To illuminate darkness, aimed upon the target,
How could one fittingly see that golden reality?

"Friends, these should be understood as a good discussion of the excellent intention, 'One should keep the excellent lamp.'"

This concludes the sixtieth chapter, teaching the lamp of the eye of yoga, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the great beings, lowly and excellent, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, put a question: "O Lord of Secrets, how are we to fully understand the intention behind the vow, 'One should go especially to what is higher'?"

When they had requested this, the Lord of Secrets said: "O friends, that one should go especially, that is excellent. It is most excellent indeed.

"That ground is as follows. The lamp called Full Illumination, well arranged through the yoga of those excellent ones who are masters of the grounds, is this:
The difference between ground and ground
Here, where there is none at all,
Comes down to higher and lower stages of self-awareness
Through differences in cutting through what is to be known.
For example, from a king's treasury
Those who want great or small means,
Through differences of outer and inner austerity,
And by the methods of work through which they enter the gates of service,
Take whatever they take according to high or low skill;
Though they all serve a single king,
There are distinctions among the several kinds of jewels,
And the great means of the nine gates are employed separately;
But one who becomes king is lord
Of all that wealth, the one who confers authority.
In unabiding, uncontrived suchness,
Where there is nothing to be known apart from it,
Through differences of skill
All the separate gates of entry are traversed.
In that single reality of it,
Through differences of high and low in the reach of skill,
Awareness appears in such a way
As distinct stations of the grounds.
Here, where everything is one,
Seeing it is discordant, through the power of that.
For example, like a source with many gates,
The grounds and liberations, each a separate gate,
Appear thus as variety within the one.
Though one trains in the grounds, entering and so on,
In whatever grounds and liberations there are,
Since the root of all is cut here,
They are one, and there is no difference at all.
It is like mountain and Meru,
Wandering ascetic and youth:
The differences in what is seen, heard and taught
Exist in that way only in terms of meaning.
Therefore, since the very ground
On which all phenomena abide is mind,
Exemplified by the simile of space,
It should be known as the ground of all.
Thus the yogin who knows this,
Skilled in the methods of the path,
Loses the path, seeks the path, and finds the path,
And so finds the shortcut with ease and is accomplished.
To find what is found and abide in it
Is the ground of yogic practice.
For all the grounds, which are perfect in oneness,
Because awareness is superior, middling or inferior,
The one appears with ten distinctions,
And for this purpose they are to be enumerated.
These grounds of abiding, holding and conduct
Are completed in a single set of ten.
On this ground of uncertainty, subject to change,
Are five yogas: contrived and coarse conduct,
Holding to modes and signs, inclination,
And well-directed aspiration;
With entering, turning back and falling,
One circles everywhere and is uncertain.
The yogin of aspiration and intention:
This is called the ground of abiding and training.
On this ground of the foundation through reliance,
The beginner yogin
Practices the training and certainty
In both pure and impure ways.
The yogin who works awaiting command:
This is called the ground of abiding and training.
On this ground of conduct of great importance,
The yoga that discerns the great families
Is the ground of abiding and training;
Moreover, on all the grounds where there is no "I,"
By the very method of cutting, training and applying,
One wages the great contest of strength between good and bad.
On this ground of continuous training,
The warmth of moving, obtaining and stability,
And likewise the great signs of moving,
Of obtaining itself, and of stability,
Are six kinds of yoga.
The ten limits of training are brought to completion,
And, like the moon waxing bright,
The wisdom of training increases.
One is not afraid of the meaning, and wonder is born.
That being who enters with a mind in training
Does not turn back to the lower realms and enters solitude;
Obstacles too arise for him.
The yoga of the secret gate of scripture,
The yoga of continuous conduct,
And the yoga of marks and signs, these three:
This is called the ground of abiding and training.
On this ground, the Treasury of Merit,
Is the yoga of continuous conduct itself,
The place of pure inclination and aspiration,
The ground that is the abode of the mind of great skill,
Mounted upon the ten limits of training as a whole.
On this ground of going distinctively through reliance,
The yogin who draws a great breath of relief,
Holding the great family, great equal perfection,
Definitive conduct, applying great skill,
Entering objects, and applying awareness,
Goes to the completion of these seven.
In the ten limits of the cause and result of training
He applies himself in the manner of habituation.
From resting, all is seen;
On the irreversible ground there is nothing to habituate.
Though these share one basis of application,
They are reckoned as arising from separate, uncommon
Obscurations, and each wisdom dawns in turn.
Therefore, within a single limit of training,
The great perfections, irreversibility and the rest,
In each truth there,
Each arising of skill dawns,
And the last is completed by self-arising itself.
For those who reach the end, who attain, and who are irreversible,
In order, through dreams, echoes,
And direct certainty, as is fitting,
The vajra prophecy of yoga is given:
"Vajra, vajra yogin,"
So he is named, and it is proclaimed
In the earthly realms and in all the buddha fields,
And he comes forth as an excellent yogin.
On this ground of the arising of the focused result
Are three yogas: the yoga of accomplishment, the great yoga,
And the yoga of arising in completion.
Born from the Victor on the path of the Victor's mind,
The Victor's son, the heir of the lineage,
All the distinctive reaches of skill complete,
He is one who engages in samādhi.
On this ground, unchanging through abiding,
The yoga of marvelous play,
The great gathering assembled, and great skill completed,
Should be known as complete in three.
The great seal is illusion;
The great king, a wheel-turning monarch,
From the station of seeing to the sixteenth ground,
Enjoys himself in the station of completion.
On this ground of the all-pervading nature of reality,
The yoga of the ornament of play,
Great bliss, and completion in the bindu
Appear as three.
If one joins object, meaning and wisdom by these,
Then through knowing, all is complete:
This is conduct that is the illusion of illusion.
On this ground mounted upon completion as a whole,
From the tenth, the last, down to the first,
The final completion is the realization of all.
In the vast expanse, the heart of space,
A hundred thousand cloudless suns spread everywhere.
Whatever appears is nothing at all;
In the essence that neither arises nor ceases,
However one looks, so it appears there;
All-pervading, boundless, cut by waves.
Moreover, through the distinction of excellence,
The yoga of the great self,
Spontaneous presence, and perfection in the vast expanse,
These three complete, are self-arising pervasion.
These grounds of yogic practice:
Where there is distinction, distinction appears;
Where there is no distinction, there is no distinction.
Yogins of high and low reach of skill
Must traverse grounds of high and low station.
O you who appear here as the supreme questioner,
Lord of Laṅkā, who have become excellent!
Here, where all yoga is applied,
In this root of the ten definite grounds,
From its root and its branches,
Twelve, seven and two are complete.
Therefore, though from the distinctions of the grounds
They are vast and many in number,
One should apply these grounds of yoga.

The source of ground upon ground,
The ground of all, is great through all;
Whoever reaches this great ground
Traverses the grounds in a mere instant.

"Friends, these should be understood as the full discussion of the intention behind the vow, 'One should go especially to what is higher.'"

This concludes the sixty-first chapter, teaching the lamp of the grounds of yoga, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the lowly and excellent ones, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, asked the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, how are we to fully understand the intention behind the vow, 'One should engage with objects'?"

When they had asked this, the Lord of Secrets fully replied: "O friends, objects are as follows. Those who wish to purify fields should engage with the object of their intention, reality itself, and with the limitless saṃsāras, the objects in which arising takes place. It is like this:
Just as launderers
Stretch out the cloth wherever it is to be cleaned,
So here, from the very first of actions,
Whatever is applied is most precious."

When he had said this, the bodhisattva great being, the Lord of Laṅkā, spoke as follows: "Lord of Secrets, since everything in every way is buddha from the beginning, there is no need for anything to become buddha now. Since that is so, there is no applying oneself to an object of intention, and nothing at all has become an object of compassion. That being so, what here has become an object of compassion? Who has become an object of compassion?"

When he had said this, the Lord of Secrets said: "O Lord of Laṅkā, it is just so. Since all are buddha from the beginning and are reality itself, there is no object of compassion and no object of intention at all; and yet, in whatever way it may be, in a manner that does not depart from the nature of reality, one should not oppose anything or be opposed to it. To act for the inner meaning itself is the great commitment, and that, since all is buddha from the beginning, there is no aim to be accomplished is great compassion. To know that there is no need to enter the excellent intention should be understood as having entered the object of the excellent intention, reality itself. Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:
Whoever abides in the excellent intention,
Since it is deeper even than the deep,
In order to resolve it from its very depths,
Does not settle down in anything at all
Together with ordinary people in the four secret domains.
Moreover, in what is fully complete,
Though there is no deed to be done by deeds,
Since its nature is to benefit others,
Here one should bring benefit.
Whatever comes to contradict that aim,
And the requisites that derive from it,
As a turtle does when something comes at its head,
Should be shed without limit or end.
Why? Because yogins everywhere
Are not other than excellent yoga itself.
If, through the power of something,
One comes to contradict the excellent aim,
Great danger discordant with it arises.
If that arises for him,
There is no protector or noble one for him apart from that;
Whatever is not held, the rope is cut.
If anything comes about for the sake of others,
From the subtlest of the subtle upward,
Each and every thing, just as it is,
Should be done diligently and with delight.
Mendicants, those who delight in begging,
And those who live their lives by begging
Should be organized into groups, sections and colleges,
And made into a field for compassion to bear its fruit.
Medicines, grains and herbal essences,
And those that are the supreme of nourishing substances,
Having been ground or made into powder,
Should be cast into rivers, waters or great lakes,
And given without attachment.
Having vowed oneself as their support,
One should found places for healing the sick, for study, practice and meditation,
Making them supreme places of resort,
And make offerings and gifts according to the wheel of time.
Wherever the scripture of the excellent ones has appeared,
Relying on all the stūpas of speech,
Through recitation, service and veneration,
One should make the paths vast and broad.
Whatever makes excellence increase:
These definitive excellent ones,
Whether they speak, give, teach or guide,
Their wisdom becomes immeasurable.
By familiarizing and familiarizing again,
In order to purify that,
Amid the many objects of thought and activity,
One should train the skills of the object itself.
Turning well the excellent wheel,
Purifying the continuum, one should look to superior wisdom.
At toll scales and on toll roads
Clearing away all the harms of hardship,
One should turn the wheel of immeasurable compassion,
And not cut off the flow of the wheel of giving.
In water, in earth, or in the sky,
In fire, or likewise in the winds,
Spreading and offering the bodily remains and the relics,
One should make a wheel of the great work begun.
For averting the deeds of immediate retribution,
Dust is shaken from the continuum of body and mind:
In the palaces of the cloud hosts of noble ones,
With bodies as many as atoms, one offers and confesses with the mind.
Burning the wood of karma, so that the Victors are pleased,
From the great path of applying great means,
One should perform the supreme fourfold fire rite;
By this the deeds of immediate retribution are averted.
The maṇḍalas from which compassion arises
Should be practiced diligently, in whatever way one is accustomed.
Of roots such as these,
Abandoning all that do not serve the aim,
One should raise a great army through the accumulation of diligence.
Thus the arising of action and the rest
Are not practiced for any other end,
Because the one-pointed, purposeful yogin
Must make the purifying of his continuum purposeful.
And if one is weak in skill and lacks it,
Gathering together all the great expenditures of accumulated virtue,
Counting here the aims of what has been accumulated,
If one brings forth the force of longing and shakes out one's skill,
Like the moon's reflection in a river when the sun has risen,
It is precious that it arises by the force of its nature.
Therefore even the smallest thing, and upward,
Should be made to serve the excellent aim.
Here, all things, held and unheld,
Stable and moving, and the rest:
If one rests the mind in suchness,
That itself is great compassion.
Lord of Laṅkā, in this way,
By the manner in which there is no object,
Whatever entering there is, from non-entering,
Is the excellent engagement with objects."

When he had said this, the beings of lowly body, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, found the excellent certainty of continuously engaging with objects.

This concludes the sixty-second chapter, on engaging with excellent objects, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again those lowly beings, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, put a question of illustration to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, how should we fully examine the intention whose pledge is, 'The mirror is to be examined'?"

When they had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "O friends, the mirror by which one comes to know what is illustrated, and by what, is this:

Appearing, imputed and arising,
Unattached, arising, appearing, pure,
Abiding and arising: the ten kinds of correspondence.
Illusion, mirage, the moon in water,
Dream, echo, optical illusion,
Firebrand and city of the gandharvas,
Emanation and reflection: the ten mirrors.
The ten corresponding objects are set forth as illustrations.
Though without entity, they appear as entities:
They are examples of what can appear as entities.

As for the nature of an illusion:
Through the exertion of some person
And the application of medicines and mantras,
It appears as anything at all, captivating the senses;
Yet in it there is no entity that comes or goes.
Just so, the nature of the secret:
From a conjunction in which causes that do not exist arise,
However it appears and arises, it has no intrinsic nature.

As for the nature of a mirage here:
At times of destruction, arising and movement,
From the nature of this and that,
Children, through the perception of being parched,
May fix upon the perception of water,
Yet how could it become a cause of quenching thirst?
Likewise, its particulars as well:
Upon what is wholly empty of intrinsic nature
Conventions, signs and names are imputed;
Since that itself is empty, it is not apprehended.

As for the moon in water here:
When the moon rises in an unobscured sky,
Its reflection appears in clear water;
In it there is no imputed entity.
Likewise, without anything being arranged,
Whatever shines clear from that very sky,
Whatever appears here is that very thing;
That itself is without entity, free of grasping and conceiving.

As for the nature of a dream:
When someone is overcome by sleep,
For a moment, an instant, a short while,
Or for years and so on, however long,
He seems to experience various pleasures and pains;
Yet just as on waking nothing at all is found,
So in emptiness, where nothing at all exists,
Whatever particular conduct one engages in,
Through nonattachment to the conduct, just as it is,
When examined, it is free of all objects of reference.

As for the nature of the arising of an echo:
In its own place, conditioned by a sound,
Just so an echo arises;
That itself is an arising from nothing at all.
The blessings of the secret of secret mantra,
For whose sake the nonexistent arises:
From nothing at all, anything at all arises;
Causes that do not exist appear when looked upon with imputation and attachment.

As for the nature of an optical illusion here:
To one with a disease of the eye, things appear;
They merely appear before confusion;
Of what so appears, nothing whatever exists.
From emptiness, the intrinsic nature free of elaboration,
Whatever kinds of miraculous display appear
Merely appear as miraculous display;
Of those appearances, none whatever exists.

From water and the clear sky
Bubbles and banks of cloud arise as distinct;
Being by nature self-arising and self-subsiding,
They are pure as water itself and sky itself.
All that appears and does not appear,
Each and every one, from that very reality
Arising of itself, just as it is,
Is certain to be purity itself.

The abode of the gandharvas is not fixed;
Wherever their forms arise,
There their city likewise arises;
In its very arising, it is not to be imputed there.
The suchness of what is just as it is:
Wherever abiding abides,
There it appears as a great immeasurable palace;
Yet it does not abide as it appears.

As for the nature of an emanation:
Arising from nothing, from one it arises as many;
What arises appears like the stream of a turning wheel;
The stream of arising appearance is never cut.
From the nature of the equality of the unequal,
It arises just as it is;
What arises here is never interrupted;
In it there is no depletion and no increase.

As for whatever reflection appears here:
In dependence on a face and a clear surface where a face arises,
Just so a reflection arises;
That itself: the two are not different.
Here, though cause and effect are separate,
The cause itself is the effect, and that very effect the cause;
Therefore in it the two do not exist;
It is suchness, just that itself.

Therefore, since all phenomena whatsoever
Are one in being just as they are,
However things appear and whatever arises,
There is no arising or appearing apart from the unmoving.

This profound and supreme secret
Is like examining the sky: its depth is hard to fathom.
Whatever one who lacks yoga conceives,
By conceiving it he will not come to realize it.
For example, if one closely examines
The core of the sun with the faculty of the eye,
One does not perceive that core;
The blazing light overwhelms and dazzles the eye.
Likewise, the childish of weak intellect,
However they examine the meaning of the essence,
Do not see the secret essence;
The weak-minded children only grow weary.

Therefore, this supreme secret,
Though it cannot be known through illustration,
Is given to the yogin as a mere partial indication,
In order to illustrate it for the deluded host.
This inconceivable nature of reality
Is left just so, free from thought.
This dharma that is left unexamined
I have not taught to the childish.
Since it transcends illustration by expression,
How could it ever be shown as 'this'?
Since it does not exist, no object to be shown is found;
For the unobservable dharma there is no path of words.
All phenomena are like space,
And space too has no characteristic.
Just as the center and edges of space are not found,
All phenomena are free of edge, center and boundary.
Whatever name gives expression to 'space',
That very expression is an entity;
Yet though the conventional name for it is spoken,
Space itself is not thereby obstructed.
Whatever is said of this as 'nonconceptual',
That very statement is conceptualization;
Yet whatever conceptualization may say,
Nonduality in it is not obstructed.
Here, all wishing for duality
And all distinguishing of center and extremes are errors.
Even the convention called 'equality'
Comes from distinguishing two, center and extremes.
Ah, brothers in Dharma, this is one:
While nothing at all exists apart from it,
Whatever is two or more is conceptualization.
Let everyone settle in this certainty.

Friends, you should understand that the supreme intention, 'The mirror is to be examined,' has thus been ascertained and discussed."

When he had said this, the beings of lowly body, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, came to settle in genuine certainty.

This concludes the sixty-third chapter, called Illustration, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the excellent lowly ones, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, asked the Lord of Secrets the question on the ninth of the ten sūtras set out here: "O Lord of Secrets, how should we fully understand the intention whose pledge is, 'One should apply what is concordant'?"

When they had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "Lord of Laṅkā, these should be fully understood as follows:

In the city of the realm of children,
Thick with the gloom of great darkness,
Here in the ever-continuing round,
The activities that bring certain entry,
Through the gathering of all the supreme intentions,
Whatever has become the domain of whomever,
However and as much as has been taught,
Should be known here as threefold:
Concordant engagement, introducing means,
And the mixing of application and conduct.
By these each and every thing, as is fitting,
Is definitely applied to the supreme aim.

Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:
Whatever concordant application one engages in,
Being certain of who is where,
One acts in harmony with those very beings.
If one shows oneself wholly in harmony with them,
That itself should come about for them.
In order to make them supreme,
Through whatever is applied in harmony with them,
One draws all their minds and leads them in.
Whatever captivates, draws and introduces the mind,
Whatever is pleasing, just that,
Applied with earnestness, is the very thing.

For example, in the realms of darkness,
Whoever has become whatever, through whatever,
That enters his mind,
And since he and it are one, not other,
Then, knowing that in him,
One gains the means to bring him under control,
And the path of words that transforms it arises.
Having passed beyond that, by entering
The mind of the supreme alone, one says, 'Ah, confusion!
Here is a fault, here a reversal,' and dedicates the rising and the rest.
Within that, this was done by him alone.
Whoever here enters into the supreme
Is fit to enter into the supreme alone.
Therefore that is its very reality;
And so, whatever its reality may be,
It nonetheless enters the supreme domain.

Lord of Laṅkā, how is this so?
If you ask what it is like:
In an instant that I saw and heard,
When my supreme spiritual friend
Was teaching this path at length
To great gatherings,
Among those who had assembled in that retinue,
Goddesses, nāginīs and low-caste women,
A circle of six maidens gathered,
Showing their joy with song and dance,
And danced their steps in pairs with great delight.
At that time, though it was not a supreme domain,
The supreme, the most excellent secret itself,
By releasing it as conduct in harmony with it,
Regarded it as the very supreme of domains.
To the host of his heirs assembled there he said:
'Ah, host of the great assembly,
All you who have arisen in my lineage and family:
Divine music, song and dance,
Melodious, graceful, charming and beautiful steps,
The means that captivate, introduce and draw the mind,
The three hundred limbs of song,
And inexhaustible steps and ways of dancing:
Gather all, delight all, apply concordance!'
Thus that supreme one spoke.

Then that very one among his heirs,
Through intelligence endowed with means,
Knowing who was to be tamed by what,
In order to apply concordance with the six worlds,
Moved in harmony from his place,
Transforming into supreme dance like lightning.
With ten million glorious hands
He filled the whole realm of space,
Dancing through the power of his steps,
And the sound of the three hundred limbs of song,
As though the three existences were filled with thunder,
Filled the six worlds without end or limit,
And so captivated and drew their minds.
The marvelous secret words of the Great Vehicle:
By transforming a single word, words without limit;
The meaning utterly beyond the path of words;
He revealed the profound domain of the buddhas.
How wonderful! Amazing, marvelous Dharma!
The meaning of marvelous play arises.
From the distinctions of concordant application
Play arises in the six worlds.
In order to cut the stream of the path of saṃsāra,
In the heart of the six worlds
The light of compassion and means radiated
And burned up all the entities of the six streams.
With wisdom unattached to modes of conduct,
In order to apply it as concordant means,
Like the conduct of the world's children
He played with the six maidens of the world.
Moving six steps, he liberated the six classes of beings;
Singing six songs, he drew in the six objects;
Proclaiming six words, he perfected the six aims.

On the ground of laziness and distraction:
The swift step of diligence, the thoroughbred's step;
The melodious song of Brahmā's voice;
The secret word of the vajra that accomplishes the aim.
On the ground of the mountain of pride:
The step of hero and of strongman;
The song melodious with a hundred thousand cymbals;
The word of calm abiding, nonconceptual space.
On the ground where all things gather:
The step of five supreme bulls at play;
Song and dance, captivating and melodious song;
The word that pulls out the stake of grasping.
On the ground where delusion gathers:
The step of a lion fiercely at play;
Song melodious as the kalaviṅka's voice;
The word clear with meaning from the very beginning.
On the ground where passion gathers:
The step of the great vajra garuḍa soaring;
Song melodious as the rumbling of great thunder;
The word of the blazing wish-fulfilling jewel.
On the ground where aversion gathers:
The step of the savage wrathful one in fury;
The song of savage rākṣasīs in fury;
The word of vajra storm and meteor.

From the marvelous secret maṇḍala
The fire of blazing wisdom arises,
Burns the firewood of the saṃsāra of the three realms,
Dries up the blood-lake of the saṃsāra of the three existences,
Destroys the mountain of pride from its foundation,
Quenches the blazing flames of aversion,
Utterly conquers the darkness of delusion,
And dries up the swamp where saṃsāra gathers.
Lord of Laṅkā, these
Are the play that entices children:
By applying what is in harmony with their share,
They are applied to the supreme aim.

Lord of Laṅkā, there is more besides:
The yogic discipline of blending by introducing skillful means
Is that, just as the world has its perverse cravings,
One carries out the distinctive conduct of the discipline.
Like the fox, the thoroughbred and the fine steed,
Like an elephant gone utterly mad,
Heroic, devouring, fierce, ferocious and wild,
Like the great sky-soaring garuḍa that heeds nothing,
Fierce and squat, a great trunk, terrifying and coiled,
Capable of anything, never turning back, the gold of the ocean,
Having a mind like a mute's, like darkness,
Unchanging like Meru, of great stability,
Like space, free of all wide and narrow,
The Fierce One with a tongue of meteoric iron,
The great Fierce One, master of the discipline,
A mind that devours, like jealous rivalry,
Steadfast in the certain pride of the elephant,
The matchless lion that roars a single sound,
Soft to the touch, keeping company with passion like a bird,
An illusion enticing in the manner of illusion,
Conceptualizing nothing, yet practicing everything,
Unobstructed devourer, swift in action, a devouring mouth,
Swift as an instant, changing like lightning,
A garuḍa guarding whatever is to be guarded,
Mastering the land, taking a bride, moving in the royal court.
The great yogic discipline
Keeps in mind the twelve and the three.
What is practiced here
Is the supreme secret means of yoga:
By introducing it into anything at all, one blends;
It is practicing all moral discipline as one.

Lord of Laṅkā, there is more besides:
The conduct of engaging through accord
Is that very thing, thus, just so:
Whatever manner resembles whatever it may be.
For those whose desire has become male and female,
Whatever appears as male and female from desiring male and female,
To appear as male and female out of the absence of male and female is the conduct of engaging in accord.
For those whom attachment to desire turns to anger,
Whatever appears as anger from desiring anger,
To appear as anger out of the absence of anger is the conduct of engaging in accord.
For those to whom attachment to desire appears as darkness,
Whatever has become darkness from desiring darkness,
To appear as darkness out of the absence of darkness is the conduct of engaging in accord.
Just so, in whatever way, likewise,
With these and the rest, without limit,
All things whatsoever appear there:
Through the conduct of engaging in accord, the aim is accomplished.

"Lord of Laṅkā, one should engage with them in accord with them." When he had said this, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, both lesser and excellent, became free of conceptualizing the view and conduct that have no final limit.

This is the sixty-fourth chapter, called "Liberation through Displays in the Play of Illusion," from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then the Lord of Laṅkā again put a question to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, how should the intention of the pledge, 'I shall heal the sick,' be understood?" When he had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this. One should heal the sick of the three cities. One should be skilled in the root of disease. One should strive in the means of purging disease. One should rely on excellent physicians. One should keep and increase the medicine of the excellent remedy. One should be skilled in compounding medicines. One should guard the regimen of medicine. One should not part from the gathering of the sick. One should cut the continuity of disease. One should become the remedy for disease.

"Lord of Laṅkā, the three cities are the city of non-appearance, the city of total appearance, and the city of half appearance. The root of disease is the thorn that arises from great darkness. The means of purging disease is bringing together medicine and physician. The excellent physician is the excellent spiritual friend. The medicine of the excellent remedy is calm abiding and insight. Compounding medicine is applying these to suchness itself. Guarding the regimen of medicine is not departing from suchness. Not parting from the gathering of the sick is not wearying of saṃsāra. Cutting the continuity of disease is the certainty that all things are that very nature. Becoming the remedy for disease is fully delivering beings from saṃsāra.

"Lord of Laṅkā, there is this as well.
On an occasion that I saw and heard,
When that excellent friend of all
Tamed the Lord of the Charnel Ground,
All the chiefs of the world assembled
And were placed in the gathering of the heirs.
From among those vast assemblies
Gathered in that retinue,
A follower of a wrong school, present there incidentally,
One who greatly practiced the discipline of medicine,
The sage Black Kāla,
Approached and made this request:
'You, great and supreme King of Medicine,
Hold the maṇḍala of medicine that conquers poison.
Since you are the enemy of every disease,
Please pacify the diseases of the thorn.'
At this earnest request,
That great and supreme King of Medicine,
With kindly regard for the sage,
Spoke what he had spoken in the gathering before:
'In the casket of the three realms,
The beings of the three existences, the great poisons,
Compounded with the elixir of the buddhas of the three times,
Complete the maṇḍala of self-arisen amṛta.'
When he had spoken this with certainty,
Black Kāla made a request:
'From this body that appears as intrinsic nature
Come the five great requisites,
The amṛta that dispels the five poisons,
The supreme medicine of the sages.'
At this earnest request,
The excellent physician spoke thus:
'Self-arisen and pure from the very beginning,
The medicine compounded from eight roots and a thousand,
The substance of the five families and the five samayas,
The ingredient accomplished in the fullness of the five wisdoms,
In order to liberate all beings of the five lineages,
Is the supreme amṛta that dispels the five poisons.'
Having spoken thus, he remained silent."

Then Black Kāla again made a request:
"Since it arises from oneself, it does not arise from another.
It is pure, being exhausted into nothing but essence and dregs.
The eight roots and the thousand branches
And the five pairs become complete."
At this earnest request,
The heir who had gathered in that retinue,
You yourself, endowed with intelligence and means,
Spoke this means of loosening attachment:
"The five substances, the later outflows,
Since they arise from the cause
That is the continuum of the elements and of food,
Do not arise from oneself; they arise from another."
When the excellent friend had said this,
Black Kāla spoke again:
"If that is how it is,
Then since it is a plain contradiction,
Where could the dharma
Called 'self-arisen, self-arisen' be found?"
When he had asked this question,
The excellent one himself answered him:
"If there is any dharma that arises
Without depending on causes and conditions,
Then since it does not arise from another and has no maker,
That dharma is held to be self-arisen."
When he had said this, in reply
Black Kāla spoke thus:
"It is not abandoned by abandoning it:
A dharma that arises continually from oneself,
Not arising from another, without cause and effect,
Whatever that dharma is, it is self-arisen."
To that the other spoke thus:
"A dharma not to be abandoned but to be adopted
Ought to abide at all times and in every way
Without meeting or parting;
If the continuum of its arising ceases, its continuum ceases."
To that, in this way,
Black Kāla answered again:
"Because it is amṛta, it is able
To perform each of its own proper functions.
In the world, whatever is able
To bring benefit is known as an elixir."
To that, here,
The supreme excellent friend answered:
"Since it is called amṛta,
Amṛta has two functions:
It heals disease and raises the dead;
But these do not have those two powers."
In reply to what he said,
Black Kāla answered him:
"Since countless victors of the past
Practiced these five disciplines as foremost,
And since they held them as the supreme samaya,
They are known to be the samaya of old."
To that the excellent one said:
"If they are certainly the samaya of the buddhas,
Then from the very beginning until completion
One must possess them without parting from them;
If the stream of outflows were cut, one would be parted from them: a contradiction."
To that Kāla said:
"Since the self is permanent,
That outflow too is permanent;
It is utterly and entirely inseparable,
So how could there be parting through its exhaustion?"
To that the excellent one said:
"Since all the outer and inner outflows,
Each and every one, are entirely exhausted,
Consider the grounds, the liberations and the activities:
Needless to say, the path of outflows, all appearance, is exhausted."
To that Kāla said:
"If that is how it is,
Then since it is a plain contradiction,
Do not those who profess that all is equal
Contradict equality itself?"
To that the excellent one said:
"Those who, not knowing what is utterly unmixed,
Fix their minds on the word 'good'
And profess Samantabhadra, the All-Good,
Become the very sense sources of darkness.
Although in total perfection all is equal,
All also appears unmixed.
Whoever does not see the meaning of total perfection
Is a traveler on the wrong path of the conduct of perfection.
The words and sounds of the vehicle of the great secret
Appear in the one as not one, each distinct.
Distinguished by their essential nature,
Whatever they are appears as definitively liberated.
If one definitely holds that the arising of outflows is the root,
Then these, just as they are, uncontrived,
Are from the beginning emptiness, free of conceptual thought:
It is simply the certainty that this is so.
If one asks why they are an elixir:
The disease of yoga is conceptual thought,
Through which one dies again and again in the depths and reaches of saṃsāra.
The root of conceptual thought
Is the five kinds of outer and inner outflows.
Since all of them, in suchness, in emptiness,
Arise free of concepts, without limit or end,
They heal disease and raise the dead."
When that supreme excellent one had answered thus,
Kāla was freed from clinging to signs;
He found that very thing there with certainty, and nowhere else,
And he himself became a great and supreme physician.

"Lord of Laṅkā, these are the deliberations on what was definitively heard: 'One should heal the sick.' This is how it should be definitively understood." When the Lord of Secrets had said this, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, both lesser and excellent, were completely liberated from the conceptualization of signs.

This is the sixty-fifth chapter, "Self-Arisen Amṛta," from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

The tenth section, placed at the end.

Then again the bodhisattvas of lesser body, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, said to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, by what name should we call this supreme secret that you yourself have so extensively deliberated? Into what is the sūtra gathered?" When they had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "O Lord of Laṅkā, this supreme secret, which I have deliberated just as it is, should be called by eight alternative names, and the sūtra should be known to be gathered into ten.

"Lord of Laṅkā, if you ask what the eight alternative names are: it is called the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas. It is also called the Essence. It is also called the Tantra of the Vajra Array. It is also called the Scripture of Perfect Yoga. It is also called the Sūtra of All-Gathered Knowledge.
